Every new leaf has depth $h_a$, and every vertex at a smaller depth has a descendant at depth $h_a$. This proves the equal-depth invariant for the next phase. After the last phase put $L=h_{2d-1}$ and let $\mathcal T$ be the resulting tree. All its leaves have depth $L$, and $L\ge1$.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
###### An obstruction to arbitrary markings.

Fix any marking $\mathcal A$ of $\mathcal T$ with at most $p_\ell$ marks at each depth $\ell$. The marks may be anywhere at positive depths; there is no restriction to colored vertices. We construct a path from the root to depth $L$ that avoids $\mathcal A$.

<a id="eq:tree-spine-budget"></a>
Suppose that a path avoiding $\mathcal A$ has reached a phase-start leaf $u_i$. The total number of marks anywhere in the first $i$ new levels of this phase is at most $$\label{eq:tree-spine-budget}
 \sum_{j=1}^{i}p_{h_{a-1}+j}
 =\sum_{j=1}^{i}2^{j-1}
 =2^i-1=M_i-1.$$ There are $M_i$ spines below $u_i$, disjoint after their unmarked starting vertex. Hitting each spine would require at least $M_i$ different marks in these levels. Equation <a href="#eq:tree-spine-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tree-spine-budget">[eq:tree-spine-budget]</a> therefore leaves one spine entirely unmarked, including its endpoint.

If $i=N$, this spine already reaches the phase boundary. If $i<N$, the number of marks in all the remaining new levels is at most $\sum_{j=i+1}^{N}2^{j-1}$. By Equation <a href="#eq:tree-continuations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tree-continuations">[eq:tree-continuations]</a>, the unmarked endpoint has one more continuation chain than this bound. The chains are disjoint after that endpoint, so one of them is entirely unmarked. Following it extends the unhit path to depth $h_a$.

The root cannot be marked. Starting there and applying this argument through all phases gives an unhit root-to-leaf path in $\mathcal T$. Thus no quota-respecting marking of $\mathcal T$ hits every complete path. The argument applies to a marking fixed on the entire final tree: each phase uses only the marks in its own block of global depths.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
###### Color counts and pruning.

At depth $h_{a-1}+i$, the only colored vertices introduced in phase $a$ are the endpoints of the $M_i$ spines below $u_i$. Spines below the other phase-start leaves have their endpoints at different depths, and their descendants are disjoint from those below $u_i$. Since the depth blocks of different phases are disjoint, there are exactly $p_{h_{a-1}+i}$ red vertices and the same number of blue vertices at this global depth.

Every root-to-leaf path in $\mathcal T$ encounters exactly one colored endpoint in each phase. It therefore contains exactly $2d-1$ colored vertices, at least $d$ of which have the same color. Let $\mathcal P^+$ be the family of complete paths containing at least $d$ red vertices, and let $\mathcal P^-$ be the analogous family for blue. These families cover all complete paths. They are both nonempty: at every phase-start leaf, one can choose a spine of either color and continue to the next phase boundary, so in particular there are paths choosing red in every phase and paths choosing blue in every phase.

In separate copies of $\mathcal T$, retain the union of the paths in $\mathcal P^+$ and in $\mathcal P^-$, respectively; these are $T^+$ and $T^-$. Retain every vertex and edge on each chosen path, including vertices with only one retained child. No edge is contracted. Hence all original depths are preserved. Every retained vertex lies on a chosen complete path, so no vertex of depth less than $L$ becomes a leaf. Conversely, every retained root-to-leaf path is the unique path to one of the chosen original leaves, and thus belongs to its specified family.

Mark all retained red vertices in $T^+$ and all retained blue vertices in $T^-$. Pruning can only decrease their numbers at each depth. The preceding color counts give the quotas in Equation <a href="#eq:tree-separate-marking" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tree-separate-marking">[eq:tree-separate-marking]</a>, and the definition of each retained path family gives at least $d$ marks along every path. All these marks have positive depth.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
###### Projection of a combined marking.

Suppose, for a contradiction, that markings $\mathcal A^+,\mathcal A^-$ satisfy Equation <a href="#eq:tree-combined-quota" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tree-combined-quota">[eq:tree-combined-quota]</a> and hit every complete path in both trees. Let $\varphi^\sigma:V(T^\sigma)\to V(\mathcal T)$ be the map identifying each retained copy vertex with its original vertex, and set <a id="eq:tree-projected-marking"></a>$$\label{eq:tree-projected-marking}
 \mathcal A=
 \varphi^+(\mathcal A^+)\cup\varphi^-(\mathcal A^-).$$ These maps preserve depths. Taking a union merges any marks projected onto the same original vertex, so for every $1\le\ell\le L$, $$|\mathcal A\cap V_\ell(\mathcal T)|
 \le |\mathcal A^+\cap V_\ell(T^+)|
     +|\mathcal A^-\cap V_\ell(T^-)|
 \le p_\ell.$$ The roots are unmarked, and thus $\mathcal A$ is a valid marking of $\mathcal T$. Every complete path in $\mathcal T$ belongs to at least one of $\mathcal P^+,\mathcal P^-$. Its retained copy is hit, so its original path contains a vertex of $\mathcal A$. This contradicts the obstruction proved above, and establishes the combined-quota assertion.

Every phase adds finitely many explicitly specified vertices and edges. There are finitely many phases, and the final pruning is determined by counting colors along the finitely many complete paths. This is a finite deterministic construction from $d$, as claimed. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:instance"></a>
### The reduction instance

<a id="stmt-thm-reduction"></a>
<div id="thm:reduction" class="theorem">

**Theorem 6** (Graph-to-packing reduction). *Fix an integer $c\ge0$ and a real constant $\rho>0$. There is a deterministic polynomial-time construction with the following property. Its input is an explicitly listed simple graph $G=(V,E)$, with $V=\{1,\ldots,n\}$ and $m=|E|\ge1$, and an integer $0\le k\le n$. Its output is an integer $B$ and an explicit list of $5B$ rational item sizes in $(1/6,1)$ such that:*

1.  *if $G$ has a vertex cover of size at most $k$, the items pack into $B$ unit-capacity bins;*

2.  *any packing into at most $B+c$ bins yields a vertex cover of size at most $k+\rho n$.*

*The running-time and output-length bounds are in ordinary binary encoding; their constants may depend on $c$ and $\rho$.*

</div>

We give the construction and its elementary properties in this section. Propositions <a href="#prop:completeness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:completeness">11</a> and <a href="#prop:soundness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:soundness">13</a> prove the two implications of Theorem <a href="#thm:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:reduction">6</a>, respectively. Vertex listing is part of the input representation, so the input length is at least $n$.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Parameters and interval geometry

<a id="eq:parameters"></a>
The integer $P_0$ below bounds a fixed score used to enforce the bin patterns. The allowance $K$ will bound items lost from those patterns when $c$ extra bins are available. Choose, once for the fixed pair $(c,\rho)$, $$\label{eq:parameters}
 \begin{gathered}
  P_0=100^{11},\qquad K=25(c+1)(1+5P_0),\\
  d\in\mathbb N,\qquad d\ge\max\{72K,120K/\rho\}.
 \end{gathered}$$ Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:competing-trees" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:competing-trees">5</a> to this $d$, obtaining $T^+,T^-$, common leaf depth $L\ge1$, and combined depth quotas $p_1,\ldots,p_L$. Put $P=\sum_{\ell=1}^L p_\ell$ and choose an integer $D_*\ge1$ bounding the number of children of any node in either tree. These quantities depend only on $c,\rho$. Set $$R=100(P+nK+1).$$ The $R$ repetitions of each incidence will make the later edge demand larger than both the local losses $P$ and the global losses proportional to $nK$.

Order the graph edges as $e=1,\ldots,m$. A *position* is a pair $r=(e,j)$ with $1\le j\le R$; positions are ordered lexicographically. Define <a id="eq:position-coordinates"></a>$$\label{eq:position-coordinates}
 \begin{aligned}
  \theta_e&=(R+1)^{e-1}-1,
  &H_{e,j}&=j(R+1)^{e-1}-1,\\
  g&=\frac{1}{16(R+1)^m},
  &b_{(e,j)}&=\frac12+gH_{e,j},\qquad \beta_e=g\theta_e.
 \end{aligned}$$ Write $H_r=H_{e,j}$ when $r=(e,j)$. For each vertex $v$, let $$\mathcal R_v=\{(e,j):v\text{ is an endpoint of }e,\ 1\le j\le R\}.$$

Each vertex label $v$ uses its own copy of the following geometry. The roots of $T^+$ and $T^-$ are represented by the closed side cells $[0,1]$ and $[4,5]$. A node at depth $\ell\ge1$ has a closed cell of width $$\lambda_\ell=g[100(D_*+1)]^{-\ell}.$$ Within a parent with left endpoint $a$, the cell of its $j$th child at depth $\ell$ is $[a+4j\lambda_\ell,a+(4j+1)\lambda_\ell]$. Finally put $\Delta=\lambda_L/10$. For each $r\in\mathcal R_v$, add the closed *job cell* $[b_r-\Delta,b_r]$ on the plus side. The *test intervals* are the leaf cells of both trees and all the job cells, each taken left-closed and right-open. Denote their union by $\mathcal I_v$. Embedding cells remain closed whenever their geometric intersection or containment is discussed.

<a id="stmt-lem-geometry"></a>
<div id="lem:geometry" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Geometry and coordinate order). *The embedding has the following properties.*

1.  *Every child cell lies inside its parent. Every nonroot cell lies in the first tenth of its root cell. Distinct depth-$\ell$ cells across the two trees have gaps at least $3\lambda_\ell$.*

2.  *The numbers $b_r$ belong to $[1/2,9/16)$, and distinct job cells have gaps at least $g-\Delta$. All the test intervals of one label are pairwise disjoint.*

3.  *For $1\le\ell\le L$, $\Delta\le\lambda_\ell/10$ and $\lambda_1+2\Delta<g$. An interval of length at most $\lambda_\ell+\Delta$ meets at most one depth-$\ell$ cell. If it contains a point of a leaf cell, that depth-$\ell$ cell is the leaf’s ancestor. An interval of length at most $\lambda_1+\Delta$ meets at most one job cell.*

<a id="eq:coordinate-order"></a>
4.  *For $r=(e,j)$, the two independent order bounds are $$\label{eq:coordinate-order}
            H_{r'}-H_r\ge\theta_e+1\quad(r'>r),\qquad
            \theta_f-\theta_e\ge H_{e,R}+1\quad(f>e).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-geometry"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-geometry</summary>

*Proof.* The right endpoint of the last child is at most $(4D_*+1)\lambda_\ell$ beyond its parent’s left endpoint. For $\ell>1$ this is less than $\lambda_{\ell-1}$, since $4D_*+1<100(D_*+1)$. For $\ell=1$ it is less than $g/25<1/10$. Sibling gaps are $3\lambda_\ell$. Cells with different parents retain at least the gap between those parents, which is at least $3\lambda_{\ell-1}>3\lambda_\ell$; the two root cells are separated by $3$. Induction proves the first assertion, including all cousin cells, and descendants remain in their depth-one cells.

Successive $H$ values within edge $e$ differ by $(R+1)^{e-1}$. The difference from $(e,R)$ to $(e+1,1)$ is the same number. Thus the first step after a position of edge $e$ is $\theta_e+1$, proving the first bound in Equation <a href="#eq:coordinate-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coordinate-order">[eq:coordinate-order]</a>. Similarly, $$\theta_{e+1}-\theta_e=R(R+1)^{e-1}=H_{e,R}+1,$$ and later edges only increase this difference. Also $0\le H_r\le R(R+1)^{m-1}-1<(R+1)^m$, proving the range of $b_r$. Consecutive baselines differ by at least $g$, so job-cell gaps are at least $g-\Delta$.

The widths decrease with depth, giving $\Delta\le\lambda_\ell/10$. Since $100(D_*+1)\ge200$, $$\lambda_1+2\Delta\le\frac65\lambda_1
 \le\frac{3g}{500}<g.$$ In particular $\Delta<g\le1/16$. Job cells therefore lie strictly to the right of $1/10$ and strictly to the left of $1$; they meet neither tree’s nonroot cells. Leaf cells have common depth $L$ and positive gaps, proving disjointness of all tests. Finally, $\lambda_\ell+\Delta\le(11/10)\lambda_\ell<3\lambda_\ell$, and $\lambda_1+\Delta<g-\Delta$. These strict inequalities and the stated gaps prove the intersection bounds. A point of a leaf belongs to its ancestor cell at every depth, so the uniquely intersected cell at depth $\ell$ must be that ancestor. ◻

</details>

The geometric separation has two uses. A local interval of length $\lambda_\ell$, even with the additional shortening $\Delta$, can intersect only one tree cell at depth $\ell$. The two coordinate-order bounds will separately control which row positions and which edge resources can be matched. We next realize these constraints through five-item bins.

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Patterns and the complete item inventory

Items have five roles: rows $X$, anchors $A$, global auxiliaries $U$, local auxiliaries $D$, and flags $F$. Their subclasses are listed in Table <a href="#tab:patterns" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:patterns">1</a>. A *tuple* consists of five distinct physical item copies. A *table tuple* has one of the displayed subclass patterns. Its kind is $\mathrm M$ for the first three rows of Table <a href="#tab:patterns" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:patterns">1</a> and $\mathrm G$ for the last row. Only the roles $X,A,D$ carry vertex labels. A table tuple is *good* when these three labels are equal.

<div id="tab:patterns">

|    Kind     |       $X$        |     $A$      |       $U$        |           $D$            |           $F$            |
|:-----------:|:----------------:|:------------:|:----------------:|:------------------------:|:------------------------:|
| $\mathrm M$ | $\mathrm{T}_{+}$ | $\mathrm{Z}$ | $\mathrm{U}_{+}$ | $\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm M}$ | $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm T}$ |
| $\mathrm M$ | $\mathrm{T}_{-}$ | $\mathrm{Z}$ | $\mathrm{U}_{-}$ | $\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm M}$ | $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm T}$ |
| $\mathrm M$ |   $\mathrm{S}$   | $\mathrm{Z}$ | $\mathrm{U}_{+}$ | $\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm M}$ | $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm M}$ |
| $\mathrm G$ |   $\mathrm{S}$   | $\mathrm{Y}$ |   $\mathrm{W}$   | $\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm G}$ | $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm G}$ |

The four table patterns, with one subclass in each role. Equal vertex labels on $X,A,D$ are a separate requirement for a good tuple.

</div>

The $\mathrm M$ patterns will compare a row’s completion with an anchor deadline; the $\mathrm G$ pattern will match a job row to an anchor key. The inventory below gives every role the same total count and gives the three labeled roles the same count at each vertex. We specify every item copy and a rational coordinate $w(i)$; the actual size will be defined afterwards. The symbols $b,z$ and the auxiliary lengths below are attributes of individual items.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
###### Rows.

<a id="eq:tree-row-counts"></a>
For each label $v$ and each node of either tree, *including its root*, create $d$ rows with baseline $b$ equal to the cell’s right endpoint and coordinate $w=b$. Their subclass is $\mathrm{T}_{+}$ on the plus side and $\mathrm{T}_{-}$ on the minus side. Add $P$ further $\mathrm{T}_{+}$ rows with baseline $1$. Thus the tree-row counts are $$\label{eq:tree-row-counts}
 t_{v,+}=d|V(T^+)|+P,\qquad
 t_{v,-}=d|V(T^-)|,\qquad
 t_v=t_{v,+}+t_{v,-}\ge P+2d.$$ For each $r\in\mathcal R_v$, create $2d$ rows of subclass $\mathrm{S}$, all with baseline $b_r$. Of these, $d$ are *short*, with $w=b_r-\Delta$, and $d$ are *long*, with $w=b_r$. All these rows have label $v$. An intended packing sends $d$ rows at each position to kind $\mathrm M$ and the other $d$ to kind $\mathrm G$. Put $$J_v=d|\mathcal R_v|=dR\deg(v),$$ the number of job rows assigned to each kind in such a packing.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
###### Anchors.

For label $v$, form a baseline multiset $\mathcal B_v$ consisting of every tree-row baseline, including padding, and $d$ copies of $b_r$ for each $r\in\mathcal R_v$. The latter are hypothetical baselines, so $|\mathcal B_v|=t_v+J_v$, rather than the total row count. For each test interval, replace $d$ copies of its right endpoint by its left endpoint. Leaf-row copies and the hypothetical job copies provide these replacements; the different tests use distinct designated copies. For each resulting deadline $z$, create a $\mathrm{Z}$ anchor of label $v$ with $w=-z$.

Set $\mathbf{1}\{E\}=1$ when condition $E$ holds, and $0$ otherwise. For $a\in\mathbb R$, let $B_v(a)$ be the number of members of $\mathcal B_v$ at most $a$. Moving an endpoint from $r$ to $l<r$ changes its prefix indicator by $\mathbf{1}\{l\le a\}-\mathbf{1}\{r\le a\}=\mathbf{1}\{l\le a<r\}$. Since the tests are disjoint, their deadline count satisfies exactly <a id="eq:deadline-prefix"></a>$$\label{eq:deadline-prefix}
 \#\{\mathrm{Z}\text{ anchors of label }v\text{ with }z\le a\}
 =B_v(a)+d\,\mathbf{1}\{a\in\mathcal I_v\}.$$ There are also $d$ *keys* of subclass $\mathrm{Y}$ for every position $r=(e,j)\in\mathcal R_v$, with label $v$ and coordinate $w=-b_r-\beta_e$. Their total number is $J_v$.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
###### Global auxiliaries.

These have no vertex label. Create $\sum_v(t_{v,+}+J_v)$ items of subclass $\mathrm{U}_{+}$, exactly $dk$ of length $1$ and the rest of length $0$. Create $\sum_v t_{v,-}$ items of subclass $\mathrm{U}_{-}$, exactly $d(n-k)$ of length $1$ and the rest of length $0$. An item of either subclass has coordinate $w=-\operatorname{len}(i)$. For each edge $e$, create $dR$ *permits* of subclass $\mathrm{W}$ with $w=\beta_e$, and $dR$ *nonpermits* of that same subclass with $w=\beta_e+\Delta$.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
###### Local auxiliaries.

For each label $v$, create $t_v+J_v$ items of subclass $\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm M}$. For each $\ell=1,\ldots,L$, exactly $p_\ell$ of them have length $\lambda_\ell$; the remaining $t_v+J_v-P$ have length $0$. Their coordinate is $w=-\operatorname{len}(i)$. Also create $J_v$ items of subclass $\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm G}$, with label $v$ and coordinate $0$.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
###### Flags.

Create $\sum_vt_v$ items of subclass $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm T}$ and $\sum_vJ_v$ each of subclasses $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm M}$ and $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm G}$. They have no vertex label and coordinate $0$.

All multiplicities are nonnegative. In particular, Equation <a href="#eq:tree-row-counts" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tree-row-counts">[eq:tree-row-counts]</a> gives $t_v+J_v-P\ge2d+J_v$ zero-length local items, while the $d$ root rows on each side guarantee enough global items for the specified length-one stocks, including when $k=0$ or $k=n$. Define <a id="eq:inventory-total"></a>$$\label{eq:inventory-total}
 B=\sum_{v=1}^n(t_v+2J_v).$$ Each role has exactly $B$ items. This is immediate for $X,A,D$ from their labelwise counts. For the other roles, use $\sum_vJ_v=2dmR$: the $\mathrm{W}$ inventory is exactly $\sum_vJ_v$, and the stated global and flag counts each sum to $B$ as well. The three labeled roles have equal inventories $t_v+2J_v$ at every label $v$. All coordinates satisfy $|w(i)|\le6$: cell endpoints lie in $[0,5]$, lengths are at most $1$, and $0\le\beta_e<1/16$.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Encoding the patterns and labels in rational sizes

Large-base aggregation of bounded integer equations is a classical arithmetic device; see Anthonisse <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Anthonisse1973](#ref-Anthonisse1973), Theorem 6 and Section 5]. A recent use in bin-packing constructions appears in Jansen, Pirotton, and Tutas <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[JansenPirottonTutas2025](#ref-JansenPirottonTutas2025), Section 3]. We give the bounded five-item version explicitly and separately control the defects allowed by extra bins.

The encoding has three tasks: keep every item above $1/6$, control departures from Table <a href="#tab:patterns" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:patterns">1</a>, and turn good-tuple feasibility into an inequality on the coordinates $w(i)$. Feasibility alone will allow some departures from the table. Conservation of the total scores will bound their number when at most $c$ extra bins are used.

For a collection of items, let $N_s$ denote the count of subclass $s$ and $N_r$ the count of role $r$. Consider the following ten homogeneous count equations: the five role equations $5N_r-\sum_sN_s=0$, in the order $r=X,A,U,D,F$, followed by <a id="eq:pattern-count-equations"></a>$$\label{eq:pattern-count-equations}
 \begin{aligned}
  N_{\mathrm{T}_{+}}+N_{\mathrm{T}_{-}}-N_{\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm T}}&=0,\\
  N_{\mathrm{Z}}-N_{\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm T}}-N_{\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm M}}&=0,\\
  N_{\mathrm{U}_{+}}+N_{\mathrm{U}_{-}}-N_{\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm T}}-N_{\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm M}}&=0,\\
  N_{\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm M}}-N_{\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm T}}-N_{\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm M}}&=0,\\
  N_{\mathrm{T}_{-}}-N_{\mathrm{U}_{-}}&=0.
 \end{aligned}$$ Here $\sum_s$ is over all thirteen subclasses. Let $\delta_j(i)$, $0\le j<10$, be item $i$’s coefficient in these successive left sides, and define its integer primary score by $$p(i)=\sum_{j=0}^9 100^j\delta_j(i).$$ The coefficients depend only on the subclass. Each has absolute value at most $4$, so $|p(i)|\le4\sum_{j=0}^9 100^j<P_0$.

<a id="eq:item-size"></a>
For vertex labels set $Q_v=3^v$. The secondary score is $q(i)=Q_v$ for an $X$ or $D$ item of label $v$, $q(i)=-2Q_v$ for an $A$ item of that label, and $q(i)=0$ on the other roles. Give item $i$ the actual size $$\label{eq:item-size}
 \begin{gathered}
  a_i=\frac15+\gamma p(i)+\mu q(i)+\nu w(i),\\
  \gamma=\frac1{1000P_0},\qquad
  \mu=\frac{\gamma}{1000Q_n},\qquad
  \nu=\frac{\mu}{1000}.
 \end{gathered}$$ Roles and labels specify the construction and remain available as metadata for its analysis. The output consists of the rational sizes of the indexed physical copies; the argument requires no recovery of this metadata from a size and no numerical distinctness of different kinds of items.

<a id="stmt-lem-encoding"></a>
<div id="lem:encoding" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Scalar encoding). *Every item size belongs to $(1/6,1)$, so every feasible bin has at most five items. For a five-item tuple, zero total primary score is equivalent to following Table <a href="#tab:patterns" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:patterns">1</a>. A feasible five-item tuple has nonpositive total primary score; if that score is zero, its total secondary score is nonpositive. If both totals are zero, feasibility is equivalent to $\sum_iw(i)\le0$. The complete item inventory satisfies $\sum_i p(i)=\sum_iq(i)=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-encoding"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-encoding</summary>

*Proof.* On a five-item tuple, every role-equation total belongs to $[-5,20]$ and each remaining total belongs to $[-5,5]$. All ten are integers of absolute value less than $100$. If their radix-$100$ combination is zero, reducing modulo $100$ makes the first total zero: it is a multiple of $100$ strictly between $-100$ and $100$. Divide by $100$ and repeat to obtain all ten equations. This argument applies to signed totals as well. The converse is immediate.

The role equations now give exactly one item of each role. With flag $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm T}$, the first subclass equation forces a tree row, the next three force $\mathrm{Z}$, one of $\mathrm{U}_{+},\mathrm{U}_{-}$, and $\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm M}$, and the last equation pairs $\mathrm{T}_{-}$ precisely with $\mathrm{U}_{-}$. With flag $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm M}$, the row is $\mathrm{S}$, the anchor and local item are $\mathrm{Z},\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm M}$, and the global item must be $\mathrm{U}_{+}$. With flag $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm G}$, the remaining subclasses are forced to be $\mathrm{S},\mathrm{Y},\mathrm{W},\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm G}$. These are exactly the four patterns, each of which satisfies every equation.

The perturbation of one item from $1/5$ is bounded by $$|a_i-1/5|
 \le\frac1{1000}+\frac{2}{10^6P_0}
       +\frac{6}{10^9P_0Q_n}<\frac1{30}.$$ Thus $a_i>1/6$ and $a_i<7/30<1$. For five items, the total absolute contribution below the primary scale is at most $10Q_n\mu+30\nu$, and $$\frac{10Q_n\mu+30\nu}{\gamma}
 =\frac1{100}+\frac{3}{100000Q_n}<1,
 \qquad \frac{30\nu}{\mu}=\frac3{100}<1.$$ An integer primary total at least $1$ would therefore make the bin’s size exceed $1$. If the primary total is zero, the same argument at the secondary scale excludes a positive secondary total. When both vanish, the five copies of $1/5$ already sum to $1$, leaving exactly the asserted coordinate condition.

Finally, the whole inventory satisfies all ten count equations. Every role has $B$ items; the tree-row and $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm T}$ counts both equal $\sum_vt_v$; each of the $\mathrm{Z}$, combined $\mathrm{U}_{+},\mathrm{U}_{-}$, and $\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm M}$ counts equals $\sum_v(t_v+J_v)$; and the $\mathrm{T}_{-}$ and $\mathrm{U}_{-}$ counts agree. Thus the primary total is zero. At each label the $X,A,D$ inventories are equal, so its secondary contribution is $(1-2+1)Q_v(t_v+2J_v)=0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
###### Polynomial construction.

For fixed $c,\rho$, the trees and all their parameters are finite constants. In particular $t_v=t$ is a fixed constant, while $R=O(n+1)$ and $B=nt+4dmR$. Hence all positions and all item copies can be enumerated in polynomial time in the explicit graph input. The denominators <a id="eq:encoding-denominators"></a>$$\label{eq:encoding-denominators}
 \begin{aligned}
  D_{\rm geom}&=160(R+1)^m[100(D_*+1)]^L,\\
  D_{\rm size}&=10^9P_0 3^n D_{\rm geom}
 \end{aligned}$$ are common denominators for all coordinates $w(i)$ and all sizes $a_i$, respectively. Indeed, $D_{\rm geom}$ accommodates $g$, every $\lambda_\ell$, $\Delta$, all nested endpoints, and every baseline and $\beta_e$; Equation <a href="#eq:item-size" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:item-size">[eq:item-size]</a> then gives the second claim. Their bit lengths are $O(n+m\log(n+2))$, with constants depending on the fixed parameters. Since $0<a_i<1$, numerators over $D_{\rm size}$ have the same bound. Integer powering, arithmetic, item enumeration, and optional fraction reduction therefore take polynomial bit time. The choice of $d$ and the finite tree construction are fixed for each reduction; $\rho$ is not a real-valued runtime input.

<a id="stmt-lem-good-anchors"></a>
<div id="lem:good-anchors" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Good anchors under an additive allowance). *In any packing of the constructed instance into at most $B+c$ bins, fewer than $K$ items lie outside five-item table tuples. At each vertex label $v$, at most $3K$ anchors lie outside good tuples. This is a joint bound on its $\mathrm{Z}$ and $\mathrm{Y}$ anchors, and therefore applies to any subset of that label’s anchors.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-good-anchors"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-good-anchors</summary>

*Proof.* If the packing has $N\le B+c$ bins, five-item capacity gives $N\ge B$. The number of unused five-item slots is $5(N-B)\le5c$. Hence there are at most $5c$ nonfull bins and at most $25c$ items in them. All full bins have nonpositive integer primary totals by Lemma <a href="#lem:encoding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:encoding">8</a>. The whole primary total is zero, so the sum of the magnitudes of the negative full-bin totals is at most $25cP_0$, supplied by the nonfull items. There are consequently at most $25cP_0$ negative-primary full bins. The number of items outside primary-zero full bins is at most <a id="eq:primary-loss"></a>$$\label{eq:primary-loss}
 25c+5(25cP_0)=25c(1+5P_0)<K.$$ Those primary-zero bins are precisely the table tuples.

In a table tuple write $v_X,v_D,v_A$ for its three labels. Its secondary score is $Q_{v_X}+Q_{v_D}-2Q_{v_A}\le0$. If $v_X>v_A$, then $Q_{v_X}\ge3Q_{v_A}$, already exceeding the negative contribution; the same argument applies to $v_D$. Thus $$v_X\le v_A,\qquad v_D\le v_A.$$ Equality of the secondary total occurs exactly when all three labels are equal.

Here is the exact conservation law that controls smaller-label imports. Fix a prefix $S=\{1,\ldots,h\}$ and a role $r\in\{X,D\}$. Let $O_A(S)$ and $O_r(S)$ count items of the indicated role and labels in $S$ outside table tuples. Let $E_r(S)$ count table tuples whose role-$r$ item has label in $S$ and whose anchor has label outside $S$. The original inventories of either role in $S$ equal $\sum_{v\in S}(t_v+2J_v)$. Every table anchor in $S$ consumes a role-$r$ item in $S$. Subtracting this consumption from the available role-$r$ items gives <a id="eq:label-prefix-conservation"></a>$$\label{eq:label-prefix-conservation}
 E_r(S)=O_A(S)-O_r(S)\le O_A(S)\le K.$$ This identity also holds for the empty prefix.

For anchors of label $v$, any unequal row label must be below $v$ and is counted by $E_X(\{1,\ldots,v-1\})$. At most $K$ table anchors of label $v$ have this defect. At most another $K$ have a smaller local-auxiliary label. At most $K$ anchors of the label lie outside table tuples altogether. Their union has at most $3K$ members, proving the joint assertion. Summing this bound over labels permits a global loss of $3nK$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### Feasibility of good tuples

Lemma <a href="#lem:good-anchors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:good-anchors">9</a> now lets us analyze an arbitrary packing through its good tuples, losing at most $3K$ anchors at each vertex. The loss is independent of the number of job positions. For good tuples, the remaining coordinate inequality has the following concrete meaning.

<a id="stmt-lem-good-feasibility"></a>
<div id="lem:good-feasibility" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (Completion and key inequalities). *A good tuple has zero primary and secondary totals. Its feasibility is therefore described exactly as follows.*

<a id="eq:completion-prefix"></a>
1.  *For kind $\mathrm M$, with row baseline $b$, deadline $z$, and associated global and local items $U,D$, define <a id="eq:completion-coordinate"></a>$$\label{eq:completion-coordinate}
            h=b-\Delta\mathbf{1}\{\text{row is short}\}-\operatorname{len}(U)-\operatorname{len}(D).$$ The tuple is feasible if and only if $h\le z$, and $h\le b$. For any collection of such row–auxiliary assignments and any $a\in\mathbb R$, $$\label{eq:completion-prefix}
            \#\{h\le a\}=\#\{b\le a\}+\#\{h\le a<b\}.$$ The last term counts coverage by the half-open intervals $[h,b)$.*

<a id="eq:key-feasibility"></a>
2.  *For kind $\mathrm G$, let its key have position $r=(e,j)$, its row have position $r'$, and its global item belong to edge $f$. Feasibility is equivalent to $$\label{eq:key-feasibility}
            b_{r'}-b_r+\beta_f-\beta_e
            +\Delta\bigl(\mathbf{1}\{\text{nonpermit}\}
                          -\mathbf{1}\{\text{row is short}\}\bigr)\le0.$$ It implies both $r'\le r$ and $f\le e$. For an exact-position long row, the global item must be a permit of edge $e$ or an item of an earlier edge. At the exact position and edge, a short row admits either a permit or a nonpermit, whereas a long row admits only a permit.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-good-feasibility"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-good-feasibility</summary>

*Proof.* The table pattern gives zero primary score; equal labels give $Q_v+Q_v-2Q_v=0$. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:encoding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:encoding">8</a>. In kind $\mathrm M$, the five coordinates sum to $h-z$, proving the first criterion. All lengths and the shortening term are nonnegative, so $h\le b$. The events $\{b\le a\}$ and $\{h\le a<b\}$ partition $\{h\le a\}$, proving Equation <a href="#eq:completion-prefix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:completion-prefix">[eq:completion-prefix]</a>, including the endpoints and the empty intervals when $h=b$.

In kind $\mathrm G$, summing the row, key, global, local, and flag coordinates gives exactly the left side of Equation <a href="#eq:key-feasibility" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:key-feasibility">[eq:key-feasibility]</a>. If $r'>r$, then Equation <a href="#eq:coordinate-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coordinate-order">[eq:coordinate-order]</a> gives a position increase at least $g(\theta_e+1)$. Since $\theta_f\ge0$, the $\beta$ difference is at least $-g\theta_e$. The final perturbation is at least $-\Delta$. The total is therefore at least $g-\Delta>0$, excluding $r'>r$ without any condition on $f$.

Independently, if $f>e$, the $\beta$ increase is at least $g(H_{e,R}+1)$. Since $H_{r'}\ge0$ and $H_r\le H_{e,R}$, the position difference is at least $-gH_{e,R}$. Again the total is at least $g-\Delta>0$, excluding $f>e$ without any condition on $r'$. For an exact-position long row, a same-edge nonpermit would leave the strictly positive sum $\Delta$, proving the resource restriction. For an exact-position, same-edge tuple, the sum is simply $\Delta(\mathbf{1}\{\text{nonpermit}\}-
\mathbf{1}\{\text{row is short}\})$, which gives the remaining assertions. ◻

</details>

For later geometric use, an $\mathrm M$ interval with global length zero and local length $\lambda_\ell$ has length at most $\lambda_\ell+\Delta$. With both lengths zero it is empty, except for a short $\mathrm{S}$ row, whose interval is its job test interval. Thus the intersection bounds of Lemma <a href="#lem:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:geometry">7</a> apply even when the row’s baseline is unrelated to the depth of its local item. Finally, a plus row has $b\le1$, while a minus row has $b\ge4$ and is never short. Subtracting a global length at most $1$ and a local length at most $\lambda_1<1/10$ leaves its completion greater than $1$. Consequently intervals on either side can cover test points only on that side.

<a id="sec:completeness"></a>
### Packing from a vertex cover

<a id="stmt-prop-completeness"></a>
<div id="prop:completeness" class="proposition">

**Proposition 11**. *If the input graph has a vertex cover of size at most $k$, the constructed items can be packed into $B$ bins.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-completeness"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-completeness</summary>

*Proof.* Enlarge the cover to a set $C\subseteq V$ of exactly $k$ vertices. Call vertices of $C$ *selected* and the others *unselected*. We first construct the $\mathrm M$ bins separately at each vertex. The local construction will also be useful when no common cover is specified.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
###### Rows and auxiliary slots.

<a id="eq:local-slot-bound"></a>
At label $v$, put every $\mathrm{T}_{+}$ and $\mathrm{T}_{-}$ row into $\mathrm M$. At each position in $\mathcal R_v$, put the $d$ long $\mathrm{S}$ rows into $\mathrm M$ if $v$ is selected, and the $d$ short rows otherwise. There are $t_v+J_v$ such rows, hence this many slots for each of $\mathrm{Z}$ and $\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm M}$. The roots and padding give the useful bound $$\label{eq:local-slot-bound}
 t_v=P+d\bigl(|V(T^+)|+|V(T^-)|\bigr)\ge P+2d.$$ In particular the number of zero-length $\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm M}$ copies is $t_v+J_v-P\ge2d+J_v$.

At a selected vertex, choose $d$ distinct plus-root rows and give them length-one $\mathrm{U}_{+}$ items; at an unselected vertex do the same with minus-root rows and length-one $\mathrm{U}_{-}$ items. Reserve $d$ zero-length $\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm M}$ copies for these funded rows. Every other $\mathrm M$ row receives a length-zero global auxiliary: $\mathrm{T}_{+}$ and $\mathrm{S}$ require $\mathrm{U}_{+}$, and $\mathrm{T}_{-}$ requires $\mathrm{U}_{-}$. Thus label $v$ has $t_{v,+}+J_v$ plus slots and $t_{v,-}$ minus slots. Globally the requested length-one counts are exactly $dk$ and $d(n-k)$; the total slots are precisely the respective global inventories. Consequently all global auxiliaries of these two subclasses can be assigned as distinct physical copies, exhausting their length-one and length-zero stocks.

In the tree on the unfunded side, choose the separate $d$-fold marking from Lemma <a href="#lem:competing-trees" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:competing-trees">5</a>. For every marked depth-$\ell$ vertex, choose one of its $d$ endpoint rows and attach a $\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm M}$ item of length $\lambda_\ell$. The depth quota supplies enough such items. These marked rows are distinct and have positive depth, so none is a funded-root row. If $s_v$ nodes are marked, then $s_v\le P$. After these assignments and the reserved root zeros, there are $t_v+J_v-s_v-d$ unassigned local slots. By Equation <a href="#eq:local-slot-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:local-slot-bound">[eq:local-slot-bound]</a>, this is at least $P-s_v$, the number of unused positive local items. Put those items in distinct remaining slots, then fill all other slots with the remaining zeros. This uses every $\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm M}$ copy. All additional positive lengths move their rows earlier and cannot remove any coverage already obtained. The same argument applies when $v$ is isolated and $J_v=0$.

Attach a $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm T}$ flag to every tree row and a $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm M}$ flag to every $\mathrm{S}$ row used here. These requests exhaust the $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm T}$ and $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm M}$ inventories after summing over labels. From now on keep each row, its two auxiliaries, and its flag together.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
###### Coverage and deadlines.

For each such group, let $b$ be its row baseline and let $$h=b-\Delta\mathbf{1}\{\text{row is short}\}
      -\operatorname{len}(U)-\operatorname{len}(D)$$ be its completion coordinate, as in Lemma <a href="#lem:good-feasibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:good-feasibility">10</a>. Always $h\le b$. Each funded-root row covers $[0,1)$ or $[4,5)$ through its interval $[h,b)$. Hence the $d$ funded roots cover every test interval on that side $d$ times, including all job intervals when plus is funded. At a marked depth-$\ell$ node on the other side, the assigned local length gives precisely its cell with the right endpoint removed. By nesting in Lemma <a href="#lem:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:geometry">7</a>, this interval contains the test interval of every descendant leaf. The $d$-fold marking covers every leaf test on that side at least $d$ times. Finally, at an unselected vertex each job has $d$ short rows in $\mathrm M$; even before any auxiliary shortening they cover its interval $[b_r-\Delta,b_r)$. Thus every test interval receives at least $d$ covering intervals in either state.

<a id="eq:complete-prefix"></a>
The baseline multiset of these $\mathrm M$ rows is exactly the one defining $B_v(a)$: all tree baselines and $d$ copies of $b_r$ at each job, irrespective of the choice of short or long rows. Recall that $\mathcal I_v$ is the union of the test intervals at $v$. For every real $a$, $$\label{eq:complete-prefix}
 \#\{h\le a\}
 =B_v(a)+\#\{h\le a<b\}
 \ge B_v(a)+d\mathbf{1}\{a\in\mathcal I_v\}.$$ The last expression is the exact number of deadlines at most $a$. Here the left-closed, right-open convention includes a shifted deadline at the left endpoint and stops counting the extra copy when its original baseline enters at the right endpoint.

Sort the completions as $h_1\le\cdots\le h_{t_v+J_v}$ and the deadlines as $z_1\le\cdots\le z_{t_v+J_v}$, retaining the identity of each copy. Equation <a href="#eq:complete-prefix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:complete-prefix">[eq:complete-prefix]</a> at $a=z_j$ shows $h_j\le z_j$. Attach the $j$th deadline anchor to the group with completion $h_j$. Only the $\mathrm{Z}$ anchors are permuted; the row, global auxiliary, local auxiliary, and flag stay together. All the anchors have the same subclass and label $v$, so every resulting tuple keeps its required pattern and equal labels. Its deadline inequality makes it feasible by Lemma <a href="#lem:good-feasibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:good-feasibility">10</a>.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
###### The remaining rows and edge resources.

<a id="eq:exact-edge-completeness"></a>
At each incidence position, pair its remaining $d$ rows with its $d$ keys, using the exact position and label. Add distinct $\mathrm{D}_{\mathrm G}$ copies of that label and $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm G}$ flags. The remaining rows are short at a selected vertex and long at an unselected vertex. For a $\mathrm{W}$ item of the exact edge, the coordinate condition in Lemma <a href="#lem:good-feasibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:good-feasibility">10</a> reduces to $$\label{eq:exact-edge-completeness}
 \Delta\bigl(\mathbf{1}\{\text{nonpermit}\}
                 -\mathbf{1}\{\text{row is short}\}\bigr)\le0.$$ Thus a long row requires a permit, whereas a short row accepts either species.

Each endpoint of an edge has $dR$ such slots. If exactly one endpoint is unselected, assign all $dR$ permits to its long rows and all $dR$ nonpermits to the selected endpoint’s short rows. If both endpoints are selected, all $2dR$ rows are short; assign the $dR$ permits and the $dR$ nonpermits to these slots in any order. Since $C$ is a cover, these are the only cases. In both cases every $\mathrm{W}$ copy is used, and all $\mathrm G$ tuples are feasible.

At label $v$ we have made $t_v+J_v$ bins of type $\mathrm M$ and $J_v$ of type $\mathrm G$. They use every labeled row, anchor, and local auxiliary once. Their flag counts are $t_v$ of $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm T}$ and $J_v$ each of $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm M}$ and $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm G}$, and all global inventories have been exhausted. Every bin follows Table <a href="#tab:patterns" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:patterns">1</a> and has equal labels, so Lemmas <a href="#lem:encoding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:encoding">8</a> and <a href="#lem:good-feasibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:good-feasibility">10</a> apply to the actual encoded sizes. The construction is a partition of all items into $\sum_v(t_v+2J_v)=B$ feasible bins. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:fractional"></a>
### The fractional packing

We construct a fractional packing of value $B$ for the four-vertex instance. Both configuration models are those of Definition <a href="#def:configuration-lp" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="def:configuration-lp">1</a>; all feasible configurations are allowed, with no restriction to the table patterns used in our witness. The matching lower bound will follow from the five-item capacity limit.

<a id="stmt-prop-fractional"></a>
<div id="prop:fractional" class="proposition">

**Proposition 12**. *For the instance constructed from the complete graph on four vertices with $k=2$, using the parameters in Equation <a href="#eq:parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:parameters">[eq:parameters]</a>, both configuration relaxations have value $B$: $$\operatorname{OPT}_{\mathrm{LP,ind}}=\operatorname{OPT}_{\mathrm{LP}}=B.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-fractional"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-fractional</summary>

*Proof.* For each label $v$, construct the local groups from the proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:completeness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:completeness">11</a> twice, once selected and once unselected. In the selected state, put short rows in $\mathrm G$ and require only nonpermits of the exact edge. In the unselected state, put long rows there and require only permits of the exact edge. Equation <a href="#eq:exact-edge-completeness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:exact-edge-completeness">[eq:exact-edge-completeness]</a> holds with equality in both states. The local $\mathrm M$ construction and deadline matching did not use neighboring states. Each state therefore gives a collection of $N_v=t_v+2J_v$ feasible bin templates, using every physical copy in the labeled roles $X,A,D$ exactly once. Fix these labeled assignments in both states, including the local auxiliaries and the independently sorted anchors. The two remaining slots in a template specify a global-auxiliary species and a flag species.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
###### Exact resource demands.

Table <a href="#tab:local-state-demands" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:local-state-demands">2</a> lists the required counts. The two $\mathrm{W}$ rows refer to each edge $e$ incident to $v$.

<div id="tab:local-state-demands">

| Species                        |    Selected     |  Unselected   |   Half of each    |
|:-------------------------------|:---------------:|:-------------:|:-----------------:|
| $\mathrm{U}_{+}$, length $1$   |       $d$       |      $0$      |       $d/2$       |
| $\mathrm{U}_{+}$, length $0$   | $t_{v,+}+J_v-d$ | $t_{v,+}+J_v$ | $t_{v,+}+J_v-d/2$ |
| $\mathrm{U}_{-}$, length $1$   |       $0$       |      $d$      |       $d/2$       |
| $\mathrm{U}_{-}$, length $0$   |    $t_{v,-}$    |  $t_{v,-}-d$  |   $t_{v,-}-d/2$   |
| $\mathrm{W}$, permit of $e$    |       $0$       |     $dR$      |      $dR/2$       |
| $\mathrm{W}$, nonpermit of $e$ |      $dR$       |      $0$      |      $dR/2$       |
| $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm T}$       |      $t_v$      |     $t_v$     |       $t_v$       |
| $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm M}$       |      $J_v$      |     $J_v$     |       $J_v$       |
| $\mathrm{F}_{\mathrm G}$       |      $J_v$      |     $J_v$     |       $J_v$       |

Pooled-item demands at one vertex in the two local states.

</div>

Each entire local state can in fact choose distinct pooled copies from the actual inventories. Both length-one pools have $2d$ copies, while a state needs at most $d$ of either. Every other vertex contributes at least $d$ root slots on each side, so the zero-length stocks satisfy $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_w(t_{w,+}+J_w)-2d&\ge t_{v,+}+J_v+d,\\
 \sum_w t_{w,-}-2d&\ge t_{v,-}+d.
\end{aligned}$$ These bounds exceed the respective largest local demands. Each permit or nonpermit pool of an incident edge has exactly $dR$ copies, enough for its local demand, and each flag pool contains at least the number required at one vertex. All pools requested by any template are therefore nonempty. Different local states may reuse copies; we will now allocate their fractional uses explicitly.

Taking half of both states at every vertex gives total demand equal to every pooled stock. Indeed, summing over the four vertices gives $2d$ length-one items on each side and exactly the prescribed zero-length counts. Each edge has two endpoints, which together request $dR$ permits and $dR$ nonpermits. Each flag has the same demand in both states, so its weighted total is also its inventory.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
###### Columns using physical copies.

<a id="eq:physical-fractional-columns"></a>
For a template $b$, let $i_b^X,i_b^A,i_b^D$ be its fixed labeled copies. Let $\mathcal U_b$ be the set of physical copies in its required global species, distinguishing side and length for $\mathrm{U}_{+},\mathrm{U}_{-}$, and edge and permit status for $\mathrm{W}$. Let $\mathcal F_b$ be the set of copies of its required flag. For every pair $(u,f)\in\mathcal U_b\times\mathcal F_b$, assign the individual-copy configuration $$\label{eq:physical-fractional-columns}
 H_{b,u,f}=\{i_b^X,i_b^A,u,i_b^D,f\}
 \quad\text{weight}\quad
 \frac{1}{2|\mathcal U_b|\,|\mathcal F_b|}.$$ Do this for every template in both states of every vertex, adding weights whenever the same configuration is generated more than once. All five physical copies in a column are distinct because their roles are disjoint. Copies within either requested pool have identical encoded sizes; hence every generated column is feasible. Coincidences of numerical sizes between different declared species do not affect their disjoint physical indices or this argument.

The columns generated by one template have total weight $1/2$. Every labeled item occurs in one template in each of its two local states, giving total coverage $1$. For a global pool $\mathcal U$, each requesting template contributes $1/(2|\mathcal U|)$ to each copy’s coverage after summing over flag choices. Its total half-weight demand equals $|\mathcal U|$, as just proved, so each global copy also has coverage $1$. The same calculation, summing over global choices, gives coverage $1$ for each flag copy. Equation <a href="#eq:physical-fractional-columns" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:physical-fractional-columns">[eq:physical-fractional-columns]</a> therefore defines a feasible solution of Equation <a href="#eq:individual-configuration-lp" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:individual-configuration-lp">[eq:individual-configuration-lp]</a>, with objective $$\sum_{v=1}^4\left(\frac{N_v}{2}+\frac{N_v}{2}\right)=B.$$

Project each individual configuration to its vector of multiplicities of equal numerical sizes, and sum the weights of configurations with the same vector. Capacity is unchanged, and summing the individual coverage equalities over copies of a given size gives its required multiplicity. This proves $\operatorname{OPT}_{\mathrm{LP}}\le B$ for the ordinary type LP, including when different species have equal sizes.

Finally, Lemma <a href="#lem:encoding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:encoding">8</a> gives $a_i>1/6$ for every item. Every feasible configuration consequently has cardinality at most five, counting multiplicity in the type formulation. Giving dual weight $1/5$ to every physical item, or to every numerical size type, is therefore feasible for the respective covering LP. Both dual objectives equal $|\mathcal I|/5=5B/5=B$. Thus both relaxations have value exactly $B$.

This construction uses feasible local bin configurations. It does not require a vertex cover of size two in the four-vertex clique, nor an integral packing obtained by making those local choices simultaneously. The integral lower bound will follow separately from Proposition <a href="#prop:soundness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:soundness">13</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:soundness"></a>
### Extracting a small vertex cover

<a id="stmt-prop-soundness"></a>
<div id="prop:soundness" class="proposition">

**Proposition 13** (Soundness). *If the instance of Theorem <a href="#thm:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:reduction">6</a> packs into at most $B+c$ bins, then its input graph has a vertex cover of size at most $k+\rho n$.*

</div>

We first use deadline prefixes to force coverage at every test point. The competing trees then force a large global allocation on one side of each vertex. Repeated edge jobs turn the plus allocations into a vertex cover, whose size is bounded by the global item inventories.

Fix such a packing throughout this section. By Lemma <a href="#lem:good-anchors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:good-anchors">9</a>, at most $3K$ anchors of any fixed label fail to belong to good tuples. This bound applies to every subset of that label’s anchors, including any chosen collection of deadlines or keys. Summing over labels gives a bound of $3nK$ when needed.

<a id="eq:sound-global-budgets"></a>
Let $\mathcal M_v$ be the set of good $\mathrm M$ tuples of label $v$. Each has a row baseline $b$, a completion coordinate $h\le b$, and the associated coverage interval $[h,b)$. Write $x_v^+$ and $x_v^-$ for the numbers of these tuples using, respectively, a length-one $\mathrm{U}_{+}$ or $\mathrm{U}_{-}$ item. Distinct tuples use distinct items, so the inventories give $$\label{eq:sound-global-budgets}
 \sum_v x_v^+\le dk,\qquad
 \sum_v x_v^-\le d(n-k),\qquad
 \sum_v(x_v^++x_v^-)\le dn.$$ All interval counts below include multiplicities of tuples.

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
#### Coverage and a forced side

<a id="eq:sound-prefix-decomposition"></a>
For a real point $a$, define $$A_v(a)=\#\{M\in\mathcal M_v:b(M)\le a\},\qquad
 C_v(a)=\#\{M\in\mathcal M_v:h(M)\le a\},$$ and let $I_v(a)$ count the intervals of $\mathcal M_v$ containing $a$. Because $h\le b$, the identity $\mathbf{1}\{h\le a\}=\mathbf{1}\{b\le a\}+\mathbf{1}\{h\le a<b\}$ holds even at tied endpoints. Consequently $$\label{eq:sound-prefix-decomposition}
 C_v(a)=A_v(a)+I_v(a).$$ The function $B_v(a)$ remains the original baseline prefix used to define the deadlines; it need not equal $A_v(a)$ in this packing.

<a id="stmt-lem-coverage"></a>
<div id="lem:coverage" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14** (Coverage at every test point). *For every label $v$ and every point $a$ in one of its half-open test intervals, <a id="eq:sound-test-coverage"></a>$$\label{eq:sound-test-coverage}
 I_v(a)\ge d-6K.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-coverage"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-coverage</summary>

<a id="eq:sound-completion-prefix"></a>
*Proof.* There are exactly $B_v(a)+d$ deadlines at most $a$. At least $B_v(a)+d-3K$ of their anchors are good. Their distinct $\mathrm M$ matches have $h\le z\le a$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:good-feasibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:good-feasibility">10</a>, giving $$\label{eq:sound-completion-prefix}
 C_v(a)\ge B_v(a)+d-3K.$$ The deadline formula uses the half-open test convention: moving $d$ copies from a right endpoint $r_0$ to a left endpoint $\ell_0$ changes the prefix by $d(\mathbf{1}\{\ell_0\le a\}-\mathbf{1}\{r_0\le a\})
=d\mathbf{1}\{\ell_0\le a<r_0\}$.

<a id="eq:sound-baseline-prefix"></a>
Let $N'$ be the number of positions in $\mathcal R_v$ with $b_r\le a$. There are $2dN'$ actual $\mathrm{S}$ rows at those positions and $dN'$ keys there. At least $dN'-3K$ of these keys have good $\mathrm G$ matches. The row-order conclusion of Lemma <a href="#lem:good-feasibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:good-feasibility">10</a> places all their rows in this same baseline prefix. Since these are distinct rows, at most $$2dN'-(dN'-3K)=dN'+3K$$ of the prefix’s $\mathrm{S}$ rows can occur in good $\mathrm M$ tuples. This inequality is valid also when $dN'-3K<0$. Other uses of prefix rows only reduce the number available. The $\mathrm{T}_{+},\mathrm{T}_{-}$ rows contribute at most their entire original baseline prefix. Since $B_v(a)$ includes exactly $dN'$ hypothetical job baselines, we obtain $$\label{eq:sound-baseline-prefix}
 A_v(a)\le B_v(a)+3K.$$ Subtracting Equation <a href="#eq:sound-baseline-prefix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sound-baseline-prefix">[eq:sound-baseline-prefix]</a> from Equation <a href="#eq:sound-completion-prefix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sound-completion-prefix">[eq:sound-completion-prefix]</a> and using Equation <a href="#eq:sound-prefix-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sound-prefix-decomposition">[eq:sound-prefix-decomposition]</a> proves the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-side-choice"></a>
<div id="lem:side-choice" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:sound-side-choice"></a>
**Lemma 15** (A large count on one side). *Every vertex $v$ satisfies $$\label{eq:sound-side-choice}
 x_v^+\ge d-6K\qquad\text{or}\qquad x_v^-\ge d-6K.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-side-choice"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-side-choice</summary>

*Proof.* A length-one global item can contribute coverage only on its own side. Indeed, a minus row has baseline at least $4$, is not short, and has completion at least $4-1-\lambda_1>1$. Every plus row, including each $\mathrm{S}$ row, has baseline at most $1$, so its interval cannot cover a minus test point.

Choose one interior point in each tree leaf cell. A tuple whose global and local lengths are both zero has an empty interval unless its row is short. In the latter case its interval is exactly its job test interval, which is disjoint from all leaf cells. For a tuple with global length zero and positive local length $\lambda_\ell$, the interval length is at most $$\lambda_\ell+\Delta\le \frac{11}{10}\lambda_\ell<3\lambda_\ell.$$ By Lemma <a href="#lem:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:geometry">7</a>, it therefore meets at most one closed depth-$\ell$ cell across the two trees. If there is such a cell, mark its tree vertex. In particular, if the interval covers a chosen leaf point, that point belongs to its depth-$\ell$ ancestor cell; the marked vertex is this ancestor. This reasoning depends on the interval’s length, regardless of the row’s baseline or depth.

Make these marks for all global-zero, positive-local tuples of label $v$, merging repeated marks at the same tree vertex. At depth $\ell$, the number of marks across both trees is at most the number of used length-$\lambda_\ell$ local items, hence at most $p_\ell$. All marks are at positive depths. The retained depths in Lemma <a href="#lem:competing-trees" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:competing-trees">5</a> ensure that every leaf has the ancestor used above.

If both counts in Equation <a href="#eq:sound-side-choice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sound-side-choice">[eq:sound-side-choice]</a> were less than $d-6K$, Lemma <a href="#lem:coverage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:coverage">14</a> would force every chosen leaf point to be covered by some global-zero, positive-local tuple. The marks would then hit every root-to-leaf path in both trees, contrary to the combined-quota obstruction of Lemma <a href="#lem:competing-trees" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:competing-trees">5</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
#### Job positions and displaced rows

The forced side at each vertex does not yet ensure that every graph edge is covered. We must connect the plus allocation to the incident jobs. A key may use an earlier row, so this connection requires an explicit count of displaced rows rather than an assumption of position-by-position matching.

For each label $v$, exclude a position $r\in\mathcal R_v$ if its closed job cell $[b_r-\Delta,b_r]$ meets a coverage interval from a good $\mathrm M$ tuple of that label with global length zero and positive local length. This definition uses closed cells; the tested coverage intervals remain half-open. Such a tuple’s interval has length at most $\lambda_1+\Delta<g-\Delta$, by Lemma <a href="#lem:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:geometry">7</a>. Distinct job cells have gap at least $g-\Delta$, so the interval meets at most one of them. There are at most $P$ positive local items of label $v$. Thus at most $P$ positions are excluded in all of $\mathcal R_v$, and each incident edge retains at least $R-P$ nonexcluded positions.

For $r\in\mathcal R_v$, let $L_{v,r}$ be the number of good $\mathrm G$ tuples whose key is at $r$ and whose row is a long row of that same position.

<a id="stmt-lem-job-matches"></a>
<div id="lem:job-matches" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16** (Long matches at a nonexcluded job). *Every nonexcluded position $r\in\mathcal R_v$ satisfies <a id="eq:sound-long-matches"></a>$$\label{eq:sound-long-matches}
 L_{v,r}\ge d-x_v^+-9K.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-job-matches"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-job-matches</summary>

*Proof.* Test at the interior point $a=b_r-\Delta/2$. Since position gaps are at least $g>\Delta$, the job baselines at most $a$ are exactly those at positions strictly earlier than $r$. Let $N'$ count those positions, and let $\xi$ count the good tuples whose key is at $r$ and whose row is from a strictly earlier position.

<a id="eq:sound-displaced-prefix"></a>
The good keys at earlier positions consume at least $dN'-3K$ distinct rows from the earlier prefix, by the same order argument as in Lemma <a href="#lem:coverage" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:coverage">14</a>. The $\xi$ current-key matches consume an additional $\xi$ rows from that prefix: their keys are distinct from the earlier keys, and a packing cannot use one row in two tuples. Among its $2dN'$ rows, at most $dN'+3K-\xi$ can therefore appear in good $\mathrm M$ tuples. Including the original tree-row prefix gives the strengthened bound $$\label{eq:sound-displaced-prefix}
 A_v(a)\le B_v(a)+3K-\xi.$$

Let $u$ count the short rows of the current position $r$ used in good $\mathrm M$ tuples. Every interval covering $a$ is accounted for as follows:

- Tuples with global length one contribute at most $x_v^+$ in total, since minus tuples cannot reach this plus-side point.

- A global-zero tuple with positive local length contributes nothing, since the position was not excluded.

- With both lengths zero, a tree row or a long job row has an empty interval. A short row covers only its own job interval, so it can cover $a$ only at the current position.

<a id="eq:sound-job-coverage-upper"></a>
It follows that $$\label{eq:sound-job-coverage-upper}
 I_v(a)\le x_v^++u.$$ A current short row with global length one may be counted in both $x_v^+$ and $u$; this only enlarges the upper bound.

Combine Equations <a href="#eq:sound-prefix-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sound-prefix-decomposition">[eq:sound-prefix-decomposition]</a>, <a href="#eq:sound-completion-prefix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sound-completion-prefix">[eq:sound-completion-prefix]</a>, <a href="#eq:sound-displaced-prefix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sound-displaced-prefix">[eq:sound-displaced-prefix]</a>, and <a href="#eq:sound-job-coverage-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sound-job-coverage-upper">[eq:sound-job-coverage-upper]</a> to obtain $$B_v(a)+d-3K
 \le B_v(a)+3K-\xi+x_v^++u.$$ Equivalently, <a id="eq:sound-xi-correction"></a>$$\label{eq:sound-xi-correction}
 u-\xi\ge d-x_v^+-6K.$$ Here the same $3K$ loss controls the entire earlier-key prefix; it is not incurred separately at its positions.

At least $d-3K$ keys at $r$ are in good tuples. Exactly $\xi$ of the good matches use earlier rows, and the row-order restriction forces every other good match to use a row at $r$. At most $d-u$ of the short rows there are available to these matches, because $u$ have already been used in good $\mathrm M$ tuples. Any other use of a short row only lowers this availability. Hence $$L_{v,r}\ge d-3K-\xi-(d-u)
          =u-\xi-3K
          \ge d-x_v^+-9K,$$ as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
#### Edge capacity and the threshold cover

<a id="stmt-lem-edge-cover"></a>
<div id="lem:edge-cover" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17** (The threshold set covers every edge). *The set $$C=\{v:x_v^+>d/8\}$$ is a vertex cover.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-edge-cover"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-edge-cover</summary>

*Proof.* Fix an edge index $e$, with endpoints $v_1,v_2$. There are exactly $2dR(e-1)$ $\mathrm{W}$ items belonging to earlier edges, counting both permits and nonpermits. This equals the total number of earlier-edge keys over all labels, since each edge has two endpoints. At least $2dR(e-1)-3nK$ of those keys are in good tuples. By the edge-order conclusion of Lemma <a href="#lem:good-feasibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:good-feasibility">10</a>, each consumes a distinct $\mathrm{W}$ item from the earlier-edge inventory. At most $3nK$ earlier-edge items therefore remain for all other uses, including good matches of keys on edge $e$. This is one global loss bound, independent of the number of earlier edges. Uses in other tuples only reduce the remaining supply.

<a id="eq:sound-edge-capacity"></a>
In an exact-position long match to a key on edge $e$, the $\mathrm G$ inequality becomes $$\beta_f-\beta_e+\Delta\mathbf{1}\{\text{nonpermit}\}\le0,$$ where $f$ is the edge of its $\mathrm{W}$ item. Lemma <a href="#lem:good-feasibility" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:good-feasibility">10</a> gives $f\le e$; if $f=e$, the item must be a permit. Thus every match counted by $L_{v,(e,j)}$ uses either one of the $dR$ edge-$e$ permits or an earlier-edge item. Summing over both endpoints and all positions gives $$\label{eq:sound-edge-capacity}
 \sum_{v\in\{v_1,v_2\}}\sum_{j=1}^R L_{v,(e,j)}
 \le dR+3nK.$$

Suppose that both endpoints were outside $C$, so that their plus counts were at most $d/8$. By $d\ge72K$ and Lemma <a href="#lem:job-matches" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:job-matches">16</a>, each nonexcluded position at either endpoint would have $$L_{v,(e,j)}\ge d-\frac d8-9K\ge\frac{3d}{4}.$$ There are at least $R-P$ such positions at each endpoint. Their total demand would therefore be at least $2(R-P)(3d/4)$. Its excess over the upper bound in Equation <a href="#eq:sound-edge-capacity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sound-edge-capacity">[eq:sound-edge-capacity]</a> is strictly positive: $$\begin{aligned}
 2(R-P)\frac{3d}{4}-(dR+3nK)
 &=\frac d2(R-3P)-3nK\\
 &=\frac{97d}{2}P+(50d-3)nK+50d>0,
\end{aligned}$$ using $R=100(P+nK+1)$ and $d\ge1$. This contradiction proves that every edge meets $C$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-cover-size"></a>
<div id="lem:cover-size" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18** (Cardinality of the extracted cover). *The set $C$ in Lemma <a href="#lem:edge-cover" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:edge-cover">17</a> satisfies $$|C|\le \frac{dk}{d-6K}+\frac{48Kn}{d}
      \le k+\frac{60Kn}{d}
      \le k+\rho n.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cover-size"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cover-size</summary>

<a id="eq:sound-large-plus-count"></a>
*Proof.* Let $$V_+=\{v:x_v^+\ge d-6K\}.$$ Since $d\ge72K$, we have $d-6K>d/8$, so $V_+\subseteq C$. The plus-item budget in Equation <a href="#eq:sound-global-budgets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sound-global-budgets">[eq:sound-global-budgets]</a> gives $$\label{eq:sound-large-plus-count}
 |V_+|\le\frac{dk}{d-6K}.$$

For each vertex in $V_+$, charge its plus count; for every other vertex, charge its minus count. Lemma <a href="#lem:side-choice" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:side-choice">15</a> makes each of these charges at least $d-6K$. Exactly one side is charged at each vertex, even if both sides have large counts. The total charges are therefore at least $n(d-6K)$, while the total of all counts is at most $dn$. Every plus count outside $V_+$ is uncharged, so <a id="eq:sound-residual-plus"></a>$$\label{eq:sound-residual-plus}
 \sum_{v\notin V_+}x_v^+\le dn-n(d-6K)=6Kn.$$ Each vertex of $C\setminus V_+$ contributes more than $d/8$ to this sum. The resulting weak bound, valid also when that set is empty, is $$|C\setminus V_+|\le\frac{48Kn}{d}.$$ Together with Equation <a href="#eq:sound-large-plus-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sound-large-plus-count">[eq:sound-large-plus-count]</a>, this proves the first asserted inequality. For the second, use $k\le n$ and $d\ge12K$ to bound $$\frac{dk}{d-6K}-k
 =\frac{6Kk}{d-6K}\le\frac{12Kn}{d}.$$ The final inequality follows from $d\ge120K/\rho$. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cover-size-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cover-size</summary>

*Proof of Proposition <a href="#prop:soundness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:soundness">13</a>.* Starting from the given packing, Lemma <a href="#lem:edge-cover" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:edge-cover">17</a> produces the cover $C$, and Lemma <a href="#lem:cover-size" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cover-size">18</a> bounds its cardinality by $k+\rho n$. All deductions used only the stipulated item inventories, the good-tuple properties, and the prescribed parameters, so they apply to every packing into at most $B+c$ bins. ◻

</details>

The polynomial construction in Section <a href="#sec:instance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:instance">3</a>, integral completeness in Proposition <a href="#prop:completeness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:completeness">11</a>, and the preceding soundness proof together establish Theorem <a href="#thm:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:reduction">6</a>.

<a id="sec:consequences"></a>
### Consequences

The reduction and fractional construction now give the unconditional configuration gap. We then use a satisfiability gap to obtain the algorithmic hardness result.

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
#### The unconditional configuration gap

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cover-size-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cover-size</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:unbounded-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:unbounded-gap">2</a>.* Fix an integer $c\ge0$, set $\rho=1/8$, and apply the actual construction of Theorem <a href="#thm:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:reduction">6</a> to the four-vertex clique with $k=2$. All parameters, including $d$, the trees, and $R=100(P+4K+1)$, are chosen as prescribed for these constants. The minimum vertex cover of this graph has size three, whereas <a id="eq:clique-cover-contradiction"></a>$$\label{eq:clique-cover-contradiction}
  k+\rho n=2+\frac48=\frac52<3.$$ Proposition <a href="#prop:soundness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:soundness">13</a> therefore rules out every packing into at most $B+c$ bins. Independently, Proposition <a href="#prop:fractional" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:fractional">12</a> gives configuration-LP value exactly $B$, for both individual-copy and numerical-type configurations. This proves Equation <a href="#eq:main-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:main-gap">[eq:main-gap]</a> for every $c$. ◻

</details>

The structural proof uses only the four-vertex clique, the finite construction, the fractional witness, and the soundness bound. The PCP Theorem enters only the algorithmic hardness argument below.

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
#### A vertex-cover gap with perfect completeness

We use the following standard consequence of the PCP Theorem. For some absolute constant $\eta>0$, it is NP-hard to distinguish satisfiable formulas having exactly three literal slots per clause from formulas in which no assignment satisfies more than a $1-\eta$ fraction of the clauses. The clause list may be required to be nonempty. Håstad’s Theorem 6.5  gives this statement with $\eta=1/16$ by taking $\varepsilon=1/16$ in its $7/8+\varepsilon$ soundness bound. In particular the YES case is fully satisfiable, as needed for the completeness direction of our reduction.

<a id="eq:clause-graph-parameters"></a>
We use a literal-slot version of the complement of the satisfiability-to-clique graph described by Karp <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Karp1972](#ref-Karp1972), p. 97]. Given such a formula with $M\ge1$ clauses, create three vertices for its literal slots in each clause and join those vertices into a triangle. Also join any two slots occupied by complementary literals, retaining only one edge when an edge is specified more than once. The result is a simple graph with $$\label{eq:clause-graph-parameters}
  n=3M,\qquad k=2M=2n/3.$$ Every vertex lies in a triangle, so the graph has no isolated vertices and at least one edge. The construction is polynomial in the explicit formula encoding.

<a id="eq:vertex-cover-gap"></a>
An independent set selects at most one slot from any clause and never selects complementary literals. Setting its selected literals true and extending the assignment arbitrarily satisfies at least as many clauses as the cardinality of the independent set. Conversely, from any assignment choose one true slot in each satisfied clause. These slots form an independent set. Thus, writing $\operatorname{MAXSAT}(F)$ for the largest number of simultaneously satisfiable clauses, $\alpha(G)$ for the maximum independent-set size, and $\tau(G)$ for the minimum vertex-cover size, <a id="eq:clause-graph-identity"></a>$$\label{eq:clause-graph-identity}
  \alpha(G)=\operatorname{MAXSAT}(F),
  \qquad \tau(G)=3M-\operatorname{MAXSAT}(F).$$ This argument also permits repeated literals and clauses. The minimum cover is $k$ in the satisfiable case and at least $$\label{eq:vertex-cover-gap}
  (2+\eta)M=k+\frac{\eta}{3}n$$ in the other case.

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### Additive hardness and the complexity equivalence

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cover-size-4"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cover-size</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:additive-hardness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:additive-hardness">3</a>.* Fix $c\ge0$ and fix $0<\rho<\eta/3$, for example $\rho=1/96$ with the preceding choice of $\eta$. Apply Theorem <a href="#thm:reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:reduction">6</a> to the clause graph and target in Equation <a href="#eq:clause-graph-parameters" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:clause-graph-parameters">[eq:clause-graph-parameters]</a>. The resulting rational list and integer $B$ have polynomial encoding length and are computable in polynomial time for these fixed $c,\rho$.

In the satisfiable case the graph has a cover of size $k$, and Proposition <a href="#prop:completeness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:completeness">11</a> gives a packing into $B$ bins. In the other case, a packing into at most $B+c$ bins would, by Proposition <a href="#prop:soundness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:soundness">13</a>, give a cover of size at most $k+\rho n$. This contradicts Equation <a href="#eq:vertex-cover-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:vertex-cover-gap">[eq:vertex-cover-gap]</a>. All constructed sizes exceed $1/6$. The promised distinction is therefore NP-hard. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cover-size-5"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cover-size</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:complexity-equiv" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:complexity-equiv">4</a>.* Suppose such an algorithm exists, and fix a nonnegative integer $c$ at least as large as its additive constant $C$. On a YES instance of Theorem <a href="#thm:additive-hardness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:additive-hardness">3</a>, the algorithm must return at most $\operatorname{OPT}(I)+C\le B+c$ bins. On a NO instance, no packing using that many bins exists. Counting the bins in the returned packing distinguishes the two cases in deterministic polynomial time. Hence $\mathsf P=\mathsf{NP}$.

Conversely, assume $\mathsf P=\mathsf{NP}$. For an explicit rational list of $N$ items and a bound $b\le N$, bin feasibility belongs to $\mathsf{NP}$: a certificate assigns each item a bin index, and every capacity constraint can be checked with exact rational arithmetic in polynomial bit time. The same statement holds when some item assignments have already been fixed. Under the assumption these decision problems are solvable in polynomial time. Binary search over $0\le b\le N$ finds the optimum. Then assign items successively, trying at most $b$ bin indices at each step and retaining one for which a completion remains feasible. At most $N^2$ further tests construct an optimal packing. The empty list is handled directly. This gives the desired algorithm with $C=0$. ◻

</details>

      "caption": "Interval coverage and deadline demand at one vertex label. Only representative nested tree cells are shown; vertical extent depicts containment. Leaf and job tests are left-closed and right-open. A row with completion $h$ covers $[h, b)$, while moving $d$ deadlines from a test’s right endpoint to its left endpoint adds exactly $d$ to the deadline prefix count on that test. When row and unshifted-deadline baseline multisets agree, covering every test at least $d$ times supplies the prefix inequalities needed to match completions to deadlines.",

      "pdf_page": 4,

        }
